## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**

The champion (score 10057.60) uses:
- State 0: Attraction (B=4), transitions on color perception (magenta, cyan) and few-neighbors condition
- State 1: Exploration (B=0, low turning rate=4), transitions on white floor, random probability
- State 2: Stop (B=1), transitions on color perception (blue) and many-neighbors condition

The 2nd place (score 10120.00) is simply a 1-state Stop machine - nearly as good as the champion! This makes sense: before the switch, stopping is rewarded; after the switch, moving is rewarded. A pure Stop machine gets ~10000 (1200 steps × 20 robots × ~50% stopped on average... actually it scores close to optimal for the first phase).

Wait - let me think more carefully. Score 10120 for pure Stop means robots are stationary almost all the time, getting ~10120 penalty in the pre-switch phase (where stationary is penalized) but very little in the post-switch phase (since they're not moving). 

Actually the scoring is: before switch → penalty for stationary; after switch → penalty for moving. So:
- Pure Stop: gets penalized heavily before switch (~500 steps × 20 = 10000) and 0 after switch → ~10000 total ✓
- Pure Move: gets 0 before switch and ~700 steps × 20 = ~14000 after switch → ~14000 total ✓

The ideal behavior: **move before switch, stop after switch**. But robots don't know when the switch occurs! The green wall light appears at switch time - that's the signal!

So the optimal strategy: move initially, then when green light is detected, stop. The champion seems to do something like this with its complex transitions, but score 10057 is only slightly better than pure Stop.

**Key insight:** Detect the green wall (color 2=green? Let me check - colors {1,2,3,4,5,6} where 2=green likely) and transition from moving to stopped.

The champion uses B=4 (Attraction) in state 0, B=0 (Exploration) in state 1, B=1 (Stop) in state 2. State 2 is Stop with transitions back to Attraction. The exploration state transitions to Stop with probability ~0.25 per step.

**Strategy for round 2:**

1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit: variants of champion with small parameter changes
3. Explore: focus on the insight - move before green detected, stop after green detected
   - Green color = clr 2 (assuming standard color mapping: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)
   - 2-state: Explore → Stop, transition when green detected (C=7, l=2)
   - 3-state with more nuance

That's 14. Let me verify the 3-state with n1 having index issue. For state 1 with 3 states, the "other" states are 0 and 2, so D=0→state0, D=1→state2. For state 0, others are 1 and 2, D=0→state1, D=1→state2. For state 2, others are 0 and 1, D=0→state0, D=1→state1. This looks correct in my machines.

For 4-state: state 0 others are [1,2,3]: D=0→1, D=1→2, D=2→3. State 1 others are [0,2,3]: D=0→0, D=1→2, D=2→3. State 2 others are [0,1,3]: D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→3. State 3 others are [0,1,2]: D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→2.

In FSM 13 (4-state): s0 n0x0→D=3→state3 ✓, n0x1→D=1→state1 ✓. s1 n1x0→D=3→state3 ✓, n1x1→D=0→state0 ✓. s2 n2x0→D=3→state3 ✓, n2x1→D=0→state0 ✓. s3 n3x0→D=0→state0 ✓. All good.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**

The champion (6760.44) is a 2-state FSM:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=10), transitions: if green detected (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) → stay in state 0 (wait, D=0 means stay?), or random (p=0.002) → stay in state 0

Wait, let me re-read. With 2 states, leaving state 0, the only other state is state 1, so D=0 targets state 1. So:
- State 0: Exploration, n0x0: D=0→state1 if green seen (p=0.9), n0x1: D=0→state1 with prob 0.002
- State 1: Stop, n1x0: D=0→state0 if green seen (p=0.05)

So: Explore until green is detected (with high prob 0.9), then Stop. If green disappears/missed, small chance to go back to exploring. This is exactly the right strategy!

**What to exploit:**
- The champion: 2-state Explore→Stop on green detection, with very low back-transition
- Variants: change rwm, change detection probability, change back-transition probability
- The #2 (7454): 3-state Attract→Explore→Stop on green - good but not as good
- The #3 (7460): 2-state Attract→Stop on green - very similar pattern

**Key parameters to tune:**
- rwm0: currently 10, try 5, 15, 20, 3
- p0x0 (green detection prob): 0.9, try 0.95, 0.85, 0.99
- p0x1 (random to stop): 0.002, try 0.001, 0.005, 0.0
- p1x0 (back from stop): 0.05, try 0.01, 0.02, 0.1, 0.0

**Exploration ideas:**
- Add a second condition to state 1 (Stop) for robustness
- Try GoToColor (green) as moving behavior before stopping
- Try having no back-transition from stop (pure one-way)
- 3-state with better structure

Note: In 2-state FSM, D=0 for transitions from state 0 → targets state 1, D=0 for transitions from state 1 → targets state 0. This is the correct interpretation.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3 results:**

The champion (6028.30) is: Attraction(att=4.5) → Stop on green detection (p=0.9), with tiny random drift (p=0.002), and Stop → back to Attraction if green seen (p=0.05).

Key observations:
1. **Attraction behavior** (att=4.5) beats Exploration (rwm=3 is 2nd best at 6051) - clustering robots together helps them collectively detect the green wall
2. **Low turning rate** (rwm=3) is better than higher rates - slower turning = more directed movement = better green detection
3. The two-transition structure (green detect + tiny random) in state 0 is important
4. Having a back-transition from Stop (p=0.05) on green is important - machines with only one outgoing from state 0 (no random fallback) scored worst
5. The random fallback p=0.002 seems optimal - p=0.005 was much worse (rank 12)

**What to exploit:**
- Champion topology: Attract→Stop on green, with random fallback
- Vary att0: try 5.0, 4.0, 3.5, 2.0
- Vary p0x0 (green detect): try 0.85, 0.95
- Vary p0x1 (random): try 0.001, 0.003
- Vary p1x0 (back-transition): try 0.03, 0.07, 0.1

**What to explore:**
- 3-state: Attract→Attract(different)→Stop, both transitioning on green
- Using repulsion in state 0 (spreads robots = more coverage = faster green detection)
- GoToColor green as the moving state (directly approaches green wall)
- 3-state with Attract then GoToColor(green) then Stop

**Important note:** The robots can see the green wall with their camera (C=7, l=2). GoToColor(green, clr=2) would make them move toward it - this could be very effective as a pre-stop state.

Let me also note: machines with only 1 transition from state 0 (no random fallback) were worst. The random fallback p=0.002 allows robots to eventually stop even if green isn't visible.

Wait - re-reading: n0x0 D=0 means "go to the other state" (state 1). So both transitions go to state 1 (Stop). The robot explores, then stops when green is seen OR with tiny probability. That means once stopped, it goes back to exploring if green is seen (p=0.05). This makes sense - after switch, green is visible and robots should stay stopped, but the p=0.05 back-transition means some briefly re-explore... Actually the p=0.05 means they re-explore, which after switch costs points. Maybe lower p1x0 is better?

Actually rank 8 had p1x0=0.02 and scored 6988 vs champion's p1x0=0.05 at 6028. So 0.05 is better than 0.02. Interesting - perhaps the back-transition helps before the switch (robots that accidentally stopped can resume moving).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key finding:** GoToColor(green, clr=2) as the moving state beats Attraction! The champion uses B=8 (GoToColor green) → Stop when green detected. This makes perfect sense: robots actively move toward the green wall, and when close enough to see it clearly, they stop.

**Parameter analysis:**
- Champion: GoToColor(green), p0x0=0.9 (green detect→stop), p0x1=0.002 (random→stop), p1x0=0.05 (stop→back to GoToColor if green seen)
- Rank 2: Attraction(4.5), p=0.95 → 5569 (close but worse)
- Rank 3-4: Attraction(4.5/4.0), p=0.9 → 5582 (tied)
- p0x1=0.002 seems optimal (p=0.003 and p=0.001 both worse)
- p1x0=0.05 seems optimal (0.08 slightly worse, 0.02 worse)

**Exploitation:**
- Vary GoToColor champion: p0x0 (0.85, 0.95, 0.99), p0x1 (0.001, 0.003), p1x0 (0.03, 0.07, 0.1)
- Try GoToColor with no back-transition or different back-transition
- 3-state: GoToColor → GoToColor(closer) → Stop

**Exploration:**
- GoToColor(green) + attraction in state 0, then stop
- GoAwayColor to spread before converging
- Different color targets for the detection (maybe cyan=6 or magenta=5 are other signals?)
- 3-state with GoToColor→Attraction→Stop

**Important:** The back-transition (Stop→GoToColor when green seen, p=0.05) is crucial. Before switch, if robots accidentally stop they resume. After switch, green is visible but 95% chance they stay stopped per step... actually they'll mostly stay stopped since probability of NOT transitioning is 0.95 per step.

Wait - after switch, green is always visible, so each step the stopped robot fires p=0.05 to go back to GoToColor. Expected steps stopped before leaving = 1/0.05 = 20 steps, then moving again. This adds cost post-switch. Lower p1x0 might be better for post-switch, but higher helps pre-switch recovery.

Let me try lower p1x0 values too.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

Champion (5191.95): GoToColor(green), p0x0=0.9→stop, p0x1=0.002→stop, back p1x0=0.01 (green detect).

Key trends:
- **p1x0=0.01** is best (lower than 0.02, 0.03) - confirms lower back-transition is better
- **p0x0=0.9** with p0x1=0.002 is still optimal combo
- p0x1=0.001 is slightly worse than 0.002 (rank 7-8 vs rank 1)
- p0x0=0.99 with p1x0=0.03 → 5328 (rank 3) - very high detection probability is good
- Using C=5 (random) for back-transition instead of C=7 (green detect) is much worse (ranks 12-13)

**The back-transition must use green detection (C=7), not random.** This makes sense: after switch, green is visible, so C=7 fires with p1x0 each step. With C=5 it would fire unconditionally, making robots move again constantly.

**Direction for round 7:**
- Try p1x0 even lower: 0.005, 0.007, 0.008, 0.015
- Try p0x0=0.9 with p0x1=0.002 (champion combo) with p1x0 variants
- Try p0x0 combinations: 0.9/0.95/0.99 with p1x0=0.01
- Try p0x1=0.0015, 0.0025

**Exploration:**
- Try no random fallback (only green detection in state 0, single transition) - risky but worth trying
- 3-state variants
- Try adding neighbor-count condition to state 0 as additional transition

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

Champion (5030.81): GoToColor(green), p0x0=0.9, p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=0.01.

Key findings:
- **p0x1=0.0015** beats 0.002 (rank 2) and 0.001 (rank 11) - sweet spot between them
- **p1x0=0.01** is still best confirmed
- p1x0=0.007 (rank 7) and 0.005 (rank 9) are both slightly worse than 0.01
- p0x0=0.9 still best (ranks 2-6 with other p0x0 values are all slightly worse)
- Single transition from state 0 (no random fallback) is terrible (rank 12, 7188) - confirms p0x1 random fallback is needed
- Extra transition from state 1 is also bad (rank 13)

**Fine-tuning around champion:**
- p0x1: try 0.0010, 0.0012, 0.0015 (champion), 0.0018, 0.0020
- p1x0: try 0.008, 0.010 (champion), 0.012
- p0x0: try 0.88, 0.90 (champion), 0.92

**The topology is settled:** 2-state, GoToColor(green)→Stop, with:
- C=7(green, p=0.9) to stop
- C=5(random, p≈0.0015) to stop  
- C=7(green, p=0.01) back to GoToColor

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

Champion (5021.12): p0x0=0.9, p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.01.

Key findings:
- **p0x1=0.001** beats 0.0012 (rank 3), 0.0015 (rank 9), 0.0018 (rank 13), 0.002 (rank 12) - lower is better, but 0.001 seems the sweet spot
- **p1x0=0.005** with p0x1=0.0015 gives rank 2 (5150) - so lower p1x0 might be better than 0.01 when combined with 0.0015
- **p1x0=0.007 and 0.008** with p0x1=0.0015 give ranks 4-5 (5170) - close
- The trend: lower p0x1 (random fallback) is better, lower p1x0 (back-transition) is better
- Try p0x1=0.0005, 0.0008, 0.001 combined with p1x0=0.005, 0.007, 0.01

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9:**

The champion from round 8 (5021.12) wasn't beaten this round - all new machines scored worse. The champion parameters: p0x0=0.9, p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.01.

Key findings from round 9:
- **p0x1=0.001** is confirmed as optimal (p0x1=0.0008 gives ranks 9-11, p0x1=0.0005 rank 12 - going lower is bad!)
- **p1x0=0.012** (rank 1, 5133) slightly beats 0.01 in this round but champion still holds overall
- **p0x0=0.92** (rank 2, 5139) is competitive with 0.9
- p1x0 range 0.005-0.012 are all close (5133-5182), 0.01 seems central sweet spot
- Going too low on p0x1 (0.0005, 0.0008) is clearly bad - confirms 0.001 is near-optimal

**This is the final round - focus on exploitation around champion + a few explorations:**

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
